=== PROMPT VARIANT ===

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N pieces placed on a number line. Initially, all pieces are placed at distinct coordinates.
The initial coordinates of the pieces are X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_N.
Takahashi can repeat the following operation any number of times, possibly zero.

Choose an integer i such that 1 \leq i \leq N-3, and let M be the midpoint between the positions of the i-th and (i+3)-rd pieces in ascending order of coordinate.
Then, move each of the (i+1)-th and (i+2)-th pieces in ascending order of coordinate to positions symmetric to M.
Under the constraints of this problem, it can be proved that all pieces always occupy distinct coordinates, no matter how one repeatedly performs the operation.

His goal is to minimize the sum of the coordinates of the N pieces.
Find the minimum possible sum of the coordinates of the N pieces after repeating the operations.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
X_1 X_2 \ldots X_N

Output

Print the minimum possible sum of the coordinates of the N pieces after repeating the operations.

Constraints


- 4 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 0 \leq X_1 < X_2 < \cdots < X_N \leq 10^{12}
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

4
1 5 7 10

Sample Output 1

21

If Takahashi chooses i = 1, the operation is performed as follows:

- The coordinates of the 1st and 4th pieces in ascending order of coordinate are 1 and 10, so the coordinate of M in this operation is (1 + 10)/2 = 5.5.
- The 2nd piece from the left moves from coordinate 5 to 5.5 + (5.5 - 5) = 6.
- The 3rd piece from the left moves from coordinate 7 to 5.5 - (7 - 5.5) = 4.

After this operation, the sum of the coordinates of the four pieces is 1 + 4 + 6 + 10 = 21, which is minimal. Thus, print 21.

Sample Input 2

6
0 1 6 10 14 16

Sample Output 2

41

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Implementation details:
- Read N.
- Read list X length N.
- Compute gaps = [X[i+1]-X[i] for i in range(N-1)].
- odd_gaps = gaps[0::2] (positions 1,3,... 0-index even)
- even_gaps = gaps[1::2] (positions 2,4,... 0-index odd)
- Sort each ascending.
- Compute ans = N * X[0].
- For odd positions: positions p = 1,3,5,... length len(odd_gaps). For idx, g in enumerate(sorted(odd_gaps)): p = 1 + 2*idx? Wait positions are 1-indexed. If idx=0 -> p=1, idx=1 -> p=3. Yes p=2*idx+1. weight=N-p. Add g*weight.
- For even positions: p = 2 + 2*idx? idx=0 -> p=2, idx=1 -> p=4. p=2*idx+2. Add.
- Print ans.

Now, final code. But before final, let's consider if input has line breaks; use sys.stdin.buffer.read().split().

Code:
import sys
def main():
    data=list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N=data[0]
    X=data[1:]
    gaps=[X[i+1]-X[i] for i in range(N-1)]
    odd=gaps[0::2]
    even=gaps[1::2]
    odd.sort()
    even.sort()
    ans=N*X[0]
    # odd positions: 1,3,...
    for idx,g in enumerate(odd):
        pos=2*idx+1
        ans += (N-pos)*g
    for idx,g in enumerate(even):
        pos=2*idx+2
        ans += (N-pos)*g
    print(ans)
if __name__=="__main__": main()

Need check if X length N, gaps length N-1. If N=4, gaps[0::2] length2, gaps[1::2] length1. Good.

Now, let's think about proof of "operation reverses three gaps" in terms of sorted coordinates after operation. We should be absolutely certain about which gap corresponds to which after operation. Let's derive with variables:
Before: y_i=a, y_{i+1}=b, y_{i+2}=c, y_{i+3}=d. Gaps: p=b-a, q=c-b, r=d-c.
Operation: move piece at b to b' = a+d-b, piece at c to c' = a+d-c.
Since b<c, b'>c'. New sorted order: a, c', b', d.
New gaps:
a to c': c'-a = d-c = r.
c' to b': b'-c' = (a+d-b)-(a+d-c)=c-b = q.
b' to d: d-b' = d-(a+d-b)=b-a = p.
So triple (p,q,r) -> (r,q,p). Yes. Note the middle gap q remains same, but note the "middle" gap after operation is between c' and b', which are the two moved pieces; it equals old q. Good.

Now, is the operation on gaps exactly "reverse triple" or "swap first and third"? Since middle unchanged, same. Good.

The set of two positions {b',c'} is reflection of {b,c}. The distance between them remains c-b? Let's check b'-c' = c-b, yes. The gap between the two moved pieces remains same. So the middle gap unchanged. The outer gaps swap. Nice.

To minimize, we want larger gap at smaller weight (larger index), so swap if r>p? Actually if r>p and w_i>w_{i+2}, swapping p to i+2 and r to i decreases weighted sum by 2(r-p). So local greedy swaps larger gaps rightward. Sorting ascending within parity achieves global min. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any issue with "sort each parity ascending and assign to positions ascending" if there are odd/even positions with weights not consecutive but decreasing. Rearrangement inequality: For two sequences sorted in opposite orders, sum of products is minimal. Since weights are decreasing with position, assign gaps increasing with position. Good.

Let's maybe prove with exchange argument: If in a parity class there are two positions p<q (so w_p > w_q) and assigned gaps a>b (larger at earlier smaller? Actually if a>b and a at p, b at q, contribution a w_p + b w_q. Swap them: b w_p + a w_q. Difference original - swapped = a w_p + b w_q - b w_p - a w_q = (a-b)(w_p-w_q) >0. So swapping to put smaller gap at earlier position (larger weight) and larger gap at later position (smaller weight) decreases sum. Thus optimal sorted ascending by position. Good.

After operation, the two inner pieces are still between endpoints, so no collision with outside. They are distinct from each other because b<c => b'>c'. So distinct. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "i-th and (i+3)-rd pieces in ascending order of coordinate" if there are exactly two pieces between them; yes by definition of sorted order, positions i+1 and i+2 are between. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "the i-th and (i+3)-rd pieces in ascending order of coordinate" after operation if the two inner pieces swap, the gap sequence reversal means the middle gap remains at same position. This implies the distance between the two inner pieces is invariant under operations that involve them as inner pieces. But if later they become endpoints in another operation, the gap between them can move? Wait if two pieces are inner, the gap between them remains same. Let's think: The gap between two specific pieces is not invariant; gap positions in sorted order can move by parity. But the gap at a given position can change. Our model is positions, not labels. Good.

After operation, the two inner pieces are still between endpoints, so no collision with outside. They are distinct from each other because b<c => b'>c'. So distinct. Good.

The inner pieces move, but endpoints stay. So the label at X1 remains original piece 1. Good.

Now, let's maybe think about if there is any hidden "pieces placed at distinct coordinates" but after operation, the two inner pieces could land on coordinates occupied by other pieces outside the quadruple? After operation, the two inner pieces are still between endpoints, so no collision with outside. They are distinct from each other because b<c => b'>c'. So distinct. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "the i-th and (i+3)-rd pieces in ascending order of coordinate" after operation if the two inner pieces swap, the gap sequence reversal means the middle gap remains at same position. This implies the distance between the two inner pieces is invariant under operations that involve them as inner pieces. But if later they become endpoints in another operation, the gap between them can move? Wait if two pieces are inner, the gap between them remains same. If later one of them is endpoint, the gap between them might be swapped with another gap? Let's think: The gap between two specific pieces is not invariant; gap positions in sorted order can move by parity. But the gap at a given position can change. Our model is positions, not labels. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "the i-th and (i+3)-rd pieces" if after operation, the two inner pieces swap, so the "i+1-th and i+2-th pieces" labels swap; the gap between them remains same but now it's between the same two labels? Actually if labels swap positions, the distance between them remains same. The gap at position i+1 remains same. Good.

After operation, the two inner pieces are still between endpoints, so no collision with outside. They are distinct from each other because b<c => b'>c'. So distinct. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with "the i-th and (i+3)-rd pieces in ascending order of coordinate" after operation if the two inner pieces swap, the gap sequence reversal means the middle gap remains at same position. This implies the distance between the two inner pieces is invariant under operations that involve them as inner pieces. But if later they become endpoints in another operation, the gap between them can move? Wait if two pieces are inner, the gap between them remains same. If later one of them is endpoint, the gap between them might be swapped with another gap? Let's think: The gap between two specific pieces is not invariant; gap positions in sorted order can move by parity. But the gap at a given position can change. Our model is positions, not labels. Good.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "the i-th and (i+3)-rd pieces" if after operation, the two inner pieces swap, so the "i+1-th and i+2-th pieces" labels swap; the gap between them remains same but now it's between the same two labels? Actually if labels swap positions, the distance between them remains same. The gap at position i+1 remains same. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    X = data[1:]

    gaps = [X[i + 1] - X[i] for i in range(N - 1)]

    odd_gaps = gaps[0::2]
    even_gaps = gaps[1::2]

    odd_gaps.sort()
    even_gaps.sort()

    ans = N * X[0]

    for idx, g in enumerate(odd_gaps):
        pos = 2 * idx + 1
        ans += (N - pos) * g

    for idx, g in enumerate(even_gaps):
        pos = 2 * idx + 2
        ans += (N - pos) * g

    print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
